I. 
Introduction 
1 
Q. 
Please identify the members of the Gas Load Forecasting Panel. 
2 
A. 
The Panel consists of Theodore Poe, Jr, Shilpa Sethia, and Thomas 
3 
Gumbley. 
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Is this the same Panel that previously submitted direct testimony on 
6 
behalf of The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY 
7 
(“KEDNY”) and Keyspan Gas East Corporation d/b/a National Grid 
8 
(“KEDLI”) (collectively, the “Companies”) in these proceedings? 
9 
A. 
Yes.  The defined terms in the Panel’s direct testimony have the same 
10 
meanings in this corrections and updates testimony. 
11 
 
12 
II. 
Purpose of Testimony 
13 
Q. 
What is the purpose of your testimony? 
14 
A. 
The purpose of our corrections and updates testimony is to provide updated 
15 
historic and forecast customer demand data used by the Companies to 
16 
prepare the forecast of gas revenues and gas costs for the twelve months 
17 
ending March 31, 2025 (“Rate Year”).  The data includes updated historic 
18 
data through February 2023 and updated forecast data up to and including 
19 
the Rate Year and the following three years Data Year 1 (ending March 
20 


--- Page 4 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecast Panel 
Page 2 of 18 
31, 2026), Data Year 2 (ending March 31, 2027), and Data Year 3 (ending 
1 
March 31, 2028). 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Did the methodology to derive the use-per-customer and meter count 
4 
forecasts change since the initial filing? 
5 
A. 
No.  The corrections and updates forecasts use the same methodology, 
6 
weather profiles, and service and revenue classes as described in the initial 
7 
filing.  The corrections and updates forecast has been updated to include 
8 
historical sales data through February 2023 instead of October 2022, as 
9 
well as updated economic data from Moody’s (March 2023).  The updated 
10 
data was used to create the new meter count and use-per-meter models 
11 
discussed in this testimony.   
12 
 
13 
Q. 
Did the methodology to account for the impact of demand-side 
14 
management initiatives and local laws/legislation change since the 
15 
initial filing? 
16 
A. 
The general methodology to account for energy efficiency savings, full 
17 
electrification, partial electrification, water electrification, Local Law 
18 
(“LL”) 97, LL 154, off-system saturation limits, and low-use upgrades did 
19 
not change since the initial filing.   
20 


--- Page 5 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecast Panel 
Page 3 of 18 
The impact of these adjustments has changed because they are either 
1 
dependent on updated forecasts or the underlying assumptions have been 
2 
updated.  The impacts and updated assumptions for demand-side 
3 
management and local law/legislation is described below.   
4 
 
 
5 
 
In addition, the corrections and updates forecast now includes the impact 
6 
of the All-Electric Building Act (“AEB Act") included in the State 
7 
Executive Budget, which is described in detail below. 
8

or more members of the Panel or under their supervision and direction:

III. 
Overview of the Companies’ Retail Gas Deliveries 
1 
Q. 
Please describe the annual forecast deliveries and the year-end 
2 
customer count forecasts for KEDNY. 
3 
A. 
As shown in Exhibit ___ (GLF-4ACU), KEDNY’s forecast of retail gas 
4 
deliveries (excluding non-core and electric generation volumes) is 1.643 
5 
billion therms in FY 2024, rising to 1.715 billion therms in FY 2028.  
6 
Natural gas deliveries are projected to increase by 18.0 million therms per 
7 
year or a compound annual growth rate of 1.08 percent per annum. 
8 
As shown in Exhibit ___ (GLF-5ACU), KEDNY’s forecast year-end 
9 
customer count (excluding non-core and electric generation) is 1,282,335 
10 
in FY 2024, rising to 1,299,748 in FY 2028.  Net meter counts are 
11 
projected to increase by 4,353 meters per year or a compound annual 
12 
growth rate of 0.34 percent per annum. 
13 
 
14 
Q. 
Please describe the annual forecast deliveries and the year-end 
15 
customer count forecasts for KEDLI. 
16 
A. 
As shown in Exhibit ___ (GLF-4BCU), KEDLI’s forecast of retail gas 
17 
deliveries (excluding non-core and large electric generation) is 1.095 
18 
billion therms in FY 2024, rising to 1.146 billion therms in FY 2028.  
19 
Natural gas deliveries are projected to increase by 12.90 million therms 
20 
per year or a compound annual growth rate of 1.16 percent per annum. 
21 


--- Page 12 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecast Panel 
Page 10 of 18 
As shown in Exhibit ___ (GLF-5BCU), KEDLI’s forecast year-end 
1 
customer count (excluding non-core and electric generation) is 631,979 in 
2 
FY 2024, rising to 653,238 in FY 2028.  Net meter counts are projected to 
3 
increase by 5,315 meters per year or a compound annual growth rate of 
4 
0.83 percent per annum. 
5 
 
6 
Q. 
Please describe the updated rate groups forecasts for KEDNY and 
7 
KEDLI. 
8 
A. 
Please see Exhibits __ (GLF-5ACU) through Exhibit __ (GLF-16BCU) 
9 
for the projected changes to the residential, commercial, and multi-family 
10 
customer classes through FY- 2028. 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
How did the Companies adjust the forecast for sponsored energy 
13 
efficiency programs?  
14 
A. 
The general methodology for incorporating energy efficiency savings into 
15 
the forecast has not changed since the initial filing 
16 
 
17 
The annual energy efficiency projections through 2025 are based on the 
18 
Companies’ achieving New Efficiency New York (“NE:NY”) goals and 
19 
no longer reflect the additional savings from the Annual Demand Side 
20 
Management (“ADSM”) Filing Update made in March 2022 (Cases 19-G-
21 


--- Page 13 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecast Panel 
Page 11 of 18 
0309 and 19-G-0310).  Post-2025, KEDNY and KEDLI’s Gross Verified 
1 
persistent savings level continues to increase by approximately one to five 
2 
percent annually until the end of the forecast horizon in 2028.  Behavioral 
3 
program contributions to energy efficiency savings through 2025 are based 
4 
on achievement of NE:NY targets.  Post-2025, behavioral savings 
5 
decrease year-over-year until the end of the forecast horizon in 2028.   
6 
 
7 
Q. 
How did the Companies adjust the forecasts based on New York City 
8 
LL 154 and accelerated electrification of new construction outlined in 
9 
the recently adopted New York State Executive Budget?  
10 
A. 
As noted in direct testimony, the Companies included the impact of New 
11 
York City LL 154 in their original forecasts. 
12 
 
13 
 
In May 2023, the New York State legislature passed a State Executive 
14 
Budget that included the AEB Act, which prohibits installation of gas 
15 
systems or equipment in new construction buildings up to seven stories 
16 
(with exceptions for commercial or industrial building greater than 
17 
100,000 square feet) beginning in 2026, and in all new buildings from 
18 
2029 onwards.  The AEB Act does not restrict or prohibit oil-to-gas 
19 
conversions in existing buildings or low-use upgrades to heating.  Like LL 
20 
154, the AEB Act allows for exemptions to certain building types, 
21 


--- Page 14 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecast Panel 
Page 12 of 18 
including manufacturing buildings, commercial food establishments, 
1 
laboratories, car washes, laundromats, medical facilities, and other critical 
2 
infrastructure. 
3 
 
4 
The Companies estimated the percentage of new construction customers 
5 
in the forecasts that would be affected by the newly enacted legislation 
6 
over the forecast horizon.  Given that LL 154 preceded the AEB Act, and 
7 
that the two pieces of legislations are not drastically different in terms of 
8 
timelines and exemptions, the Companies continued to model the impact 
9 
of the legislation in KEDNY and the Rockaways in KEDLI’s service 
10 
territory per LL 154 specifications.  The remaining portion of KEDLI’s 
11 
service territory is modeled in accordance with the requirements of the 
12 
AEB Act.  Overall, by FY 2028 in KEDNY and KEDLI, about 40 to 50 
13 
percent of the forecast annual meter growth in the residential, commercial, 
14 
and multi-family customer classes from new construction will be affected 
15 
by the restrictions in LL 154 and the AEB Act. 
16 
 
17 
Q. 
How did the Companies adjust their forecasts for New York City LL 
18 
97?  
19 
A. 
The Company’s assumptions and methodology for LL 97 have not 
20 
changed since the initial filing.   
21 


--- Page 15 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecast Panel 
Page 13 of 18 
Q. 
How did the Companies adjust the forecasts for sponsored heat 
1 
electrification programs?  
2 
A. 
The Companies’ methodologies of accounting for electrification have not 
3 
changed since the initial filing.  However, the Companies received updated 
4 
electrification outlooks from the electric distribution companies in the 
5 
KEDNY and KEDLI service territories – Consolidated Edison Company 
6 
of New York, Inc. (“Con Edison”) and the Long Island Power Authority 
7 
(“LIPA”) (administered by PSEG Long Island (“PSEG-LI”)). The updated 
8 
outlooks were used as a foundation to the electrification assumptions in 
9 
this forecast. A net adjustment was made to forecast customer counts for 
10 
demand-side management electrification only if the projected heat pump 
11 
installations were greater than customer losses from the new legislation.   
12 
 
13 
Q. 
How did the Companies adjust the forecasts for water heating 
14 
electrification?  
15 
A. 
In KEDNY, expected annual savings from electrification of water heating  
16 
were developed from Con Edison’s forecast electrification of non-space-
17 
heating systems.  The water heating electrification savings were only 
18 
explicitly adjusted if they were not captured by Con Edison’s space heating 
19 
savings projections.  Explicit adjustments were made for residential, 
20 
commercial, and multi-family customer classes in KEDNY. 
21 


--- Page 16 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecast Panel 
Page 14 of 18 
In KEDLI, PSEG-LI provided projected number of heat pump water 
1 
heaters (“HPWH”) for each customer group.  If the meter loss due to full 
2 
electrification was greater than the projected number of HPWH on 
3 
cumulative basis, no explicit adjustments were made for HPWHs.  The 
4 
annual savings from electrification of water heating were obtained by 
5 
multiplying PSEG-LI’s projected number of HPWH, net of the number of 
6 
full heat pumps, by the average dekatherms/HPWH, which are 8.2 per 
7 
year.  These expected annual savings from the HPWH installations were 
8 
subtracted from commercial class gas volumes in KEDLI.   
9 
 
10 
Q. 
How did the Companies adjust the forecasts for market saturation of 
11 
oil-to-gas conversions?  
12 
A. 
In their corrections and updates forecasts, the Companies have assumed 
13 
restrictions on residential, commercial, and multi-family growth from oil-
14 
to-gas conversions (for structures that are not currently gas customers).  A 
15 
third-party marketing database was used to identify the location of every 
16 
non-customer structure in the Companies’ service territories.  ArcGIS 
17 
software was used to calculate the distance from each structure to the 
18 
nearest gas main.  The Companies determined the propensity to convert to 
19 
gas by distance and used this information along with the non-customer 
20 


--- Page 17 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecast Panel 
Page 15 of 18 
structure location data to estimate an upper limit on future oil-to-gas 
1 
conversions. 
2 
 
3 
 
Based on the Companies’ analysis, the limit on oil-to-gas conversion 
4 
growth is not reached in the forecast period of this testimony in either 
5 
KEDLI or KEDNY.  In KEDNY, residential-heating meter growth from 
6 
oil-to-gas conversions begins to slow in FY 2028 as the market starts to 
7 
saturate, but the actual ceiling is projected to not be reached until the mid-
8 
2030s.   
9 
 
10 
Q. 
What is the projected impact of demand-side management initiatives 
11 
and local laws on the forecast customer counts?  
12 
A. 
The customer count forecasts reflect the impact of full electrification from 
13 
Con Edison’s demand-side management programs, LL 154, the AEB Act, 
14 
as well as slower growth from off-system conversions as the market begins 
15 
to saturate.   
16 
 
17 
By the end of FY 2028, electrification and slower growth from residential 
18 
off-system conversions decrease KEDNY’s total meter count by 0.62 
19 
percent, or 8,114 meters.  Residential heating customers account for 85 
20 


--- Page 18 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecast Panel 
Page 16 of 18 
percent of the total meter loss from electrification (Exhibit ___ (GLF-
1 
15ACU)).   
2 
 
3 
By the end of FY 2028, electrification from LL 154 and demand side 
4 
management programs decrease KEDLI’s total meter count by 0.35 
5 
percent, or 2,318 meters.  PSEG-LI’s electrification projections call for 
6 
larger amounts of partial heat pumps compared to full heat pumps, 
7 
resulting in lower meter loss from electrification in KEDLI as compared 
8 
to KEDNY.  Off-system saturation is not reached in the forecast horizon 
9 
in KEDLI.   
10 
 
11 
Q. 
What is the projected impact of demand-side management initiatives 
12 
and local laws on the forecast volumes in KEDNY?  
13 
A. 
The forecast retail volumes are impacted by full electrification, partial 
14 
electrification, LL 97, LL 154, the AEB Act, off-system market saturation, 
15 
and acceleration in the Companies’ energy efficiency programs.  By FY 
16 
2028, these factors reduced projected retail volumes by 121.3 million 
17 
therms as shown in Exhibit ___ (GLF-16ACU).   
18 
 
19 
The largest impact on retail volumes come from projected savings from 
20 
partial electrification at 37.7 million therms through FY 2028.  The second 
21 


--- Page 19 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecast Panel 
Page 17 of 18 
largest impact on retail volumes comes from projected savings from 
1 
KEDNY’s energy efficiency initiatives at 34.0 million therms through FY 
2 
2028.  It is important to note that this impact reflects only the savings 
3 
above the historical trend of energy efficiency that is built into KEDNY’s 
4 
historical sales data.  LL 97 reduces special contract (Other rate group) 
5 
customers’ load by 30.0 million therms by FY 2028 to ensure these 
6 
customers, on average, comply with emission limits.  Meter loss from 
7 
demand-side management electrification and the gas bans have the second 
8 
largest impact at 17.2 million therms by FY 2028.  Water heating 
9 
electrification results in 1.9 million therms in savings by FY 2028.  Lastly, 
10 
slower growth from off-system conversions due to market saturation 
11 
reduces load by 0.47 million therms in FY 2028 as presented in Exhibit 
12 
___ (GLF-16ACU). 
13 
 
14 
Q. 
What is the projected impact of demand-side management initiatives 
15 
and local laws on the forecast volumes in KEDLI?  
16 
A. 
The forecast retail volumes are impacted by full electrification, partial 
17 
electrification, water heating electrification, LL 97, LL 154, the AEB Act, 
18 
and acceleration in the KEDLI’s energy efficiency programs.  By FY 2028, 
19 
these factors reduce projected retail volumes by 34.8 million therms 
20 
(Exhibit ___ (GLF-16BCU)).   
21 


--- Page 20 ---
Testimony of Gas Load Forecast Panel 
Page 18 of 18 
The largest impact on retail volumes comes from projected savings from 
1 
KEDLI’s energy efficiency initiatives at 20.2 million therms through FY 
2 
2028.  It is important to note that this impact reflects only the savings 
3 
above the historical trend of energy efficiency that is built into the 
4 
KEDLI’s historical sales data.  Partial electrification has the second largest 
5 
impact at 9.6 million therms by FY 2028.  Full electrification from PSEG-
6 
LI’s demand-side management program and the gas bans reduce load 
7 
further by 3.7 million therms in FY 2028.  LL 97 reduces customers’ load 
8 
on special contracts (Other rate group) by 1.2 million therms in FY 2028 
9 
to ensure these customers comply with emission limits on average.  Water 
10 
heating electrification reduces load by 0.07 million therms by FY 2028 as 
11 
presented in Exhibit ___ (GLF-16BCU).     
12 
IV. 
Conclusion 
13 
Q. 
Does this conclude your testimony? 
14 
A. 
Yes, it does.
